2nd Test Cape Town
A number of Sutton Coldfield CC visited Cape Town for the 2nd Test between South Africa and England.  The party included the Chairman, Colin Greetham; Mike Hepburn; John Howard; James Ross; Paul Greetham and guest Danny Longhorne (FOAS CC); joining up with our overseas player Dan Childs (South African).  Dan was involved in an accident with John Howard as passenger after a major collision with a rogue driver in the Cape. Thankfully for both the driver (Dan) and his passenger (SCCC Cricket Chairman John Howard) survived the impact with the other vehicle, resulted in nothing more serious than a multitude of cuts and bruises.

Annual Dinner 2016
Another excellent and well attended with over 100 members and their guests attended the SCCC Annual Dinner, which took place on Friday 5th February 2016 enjoyed a great evening of celebrations at Moor Hall Golf Club with Andy Biddle saying grace in a humorist manner.
The assembled gathering enjoyed the presentation of a number of awards carried out by Guest of Honour M.J.K. Smith OBE, who was probably the best liked Captain of Oxford University, Warwickshire CCC and of England MJK in his prime faced the most hostile line-up of test bowlers playing for the West Indies and Australia that cricket supporters have probably ever had the fortune to see playing the game at the highest level. MJK’s final test batting average bears testimony to a rare talent, especially for a player who played at the highest level whilst wearing glasses.
Guest Speaker for the evening was Neil Smith, the son of MJK, who was also Captain of Warwickshire during his playing career – creating with his Father an almost unique double about which the statisticians amongst our readership will probably argue for a prolonged period.  Neil happily answered questions posed by Paul Greetham about his experiences during his professional playing career and his reminiscences of a number of Warwickshire players including the great Brian Lara and fast bowling legend Allan Donald.
The evening was rounded off by top comedian Ian Richards, King Rat of the Water Rats who also conducted an auction of a number of artifacts on behalf of SCCC.
Four awards were made as follows: -
Junior player of the year.......James Lunn
Carol Hansford Trophy.........Henry Roper
Terry Metthews Award……..Jacob Squire - Wood
Clubman of the year………..Graham Clark

2016 Club Membership
The rules of the Birmingham and District Premier Cricket League together with those of the Warwickshire Cricket League require every player in either of the leagues to be a fully paid-up member of the Club which that player is representing.  In addition, since SCCC is a ClubMark club, we are also obliged to only select eligible players i.e. fully paid up during the current playing year.  In addition to add to the problem with our selection process, SCCC also has a responsibility in respect of the Insurance cover that is provided for all players, whether junior or senior - the Club’s Insurance policy does not apply to any non-members who may wish to play for SCCC.  Therefore, it is essential that all players of whatever age are Club Members.
To ensure that every player is a legitimate Club member (each year), there is a requirement for everyone to complete a membership form as soon as possible (remember the SCCC membership year runs from 1st January each year to the 31st December).  At the pre-season senior players’ meeting, everyone will be handed a membership form which SCCC requires be filled out and handed back to a Club Official, together with the correct annual subscription or the information required for paying by staggered payments. Anyone not at the pre-season meeting will be handed a membership form by a Club Official before the season starts.
Who Runs the Rectory Park Facility?
I was asked the above question recently by a Club Member who obviously thought that the Cricket Club actually ran the entire facility.  He was wrong in that assumption because the two Constituent Clubs (Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club and Sutton Coldfield Hockey Club) both have a say in what actually happens in and around the building.
It is true to say that during the summer months the Cricket section has control of what happens in and around the club but not the running of the actual establishment itself. The running of the bar, any stock held for sale over the bar, any repairs to the building structure, and the lease of the ground from Birmingham City Council amongst other things are all controlled by a Company Limited by Guarantee which in turn is controlled by a group of 5 members drawn from both Constituent Clubs called the General Purposes Committee (GPC) plus the officers elected annually at the meeting of the joint club’s AGM.
La Manga T20 Success
A majority of the SCCC 1st XI and a number of Club supporters traveled to the Spanish sporting resort of La Manga on 7th April for four days tour to take part in a T20 cricket tournament. There are ten teams entered in the tournament including at least two representative sides, so the standard of cricket will be exceptionally high.
A record of five wins from five games in the La Manga T20 competition speaks for itself. The trip to La Manga by an SCCC XI proved to be an excellent precursor to the 2016 season, with the SCCC XI bringing home another trophy for the Cabinet.
There were two pools comprising in Pool 1: Freshfields CC; Findon C.C.; Royal Engineers C.C. and SCCC and in Pool 2: Roehampton; Lichfield C.C., a Spanish national side and Waltham & Rosedale C.C.
In the first game on Friday 8th April SCCC beat Freshfields by 50 runs with Tom Eustace top scoring with 68. In the second game on Saturday 9th April SCCC overcame Findon CC scoring 222 for 3 off 20 overs with Pete McKay scoring 126* and Lee Thomason 68* whilst sharing a partnership of 199. In the second Saturday game SCCC beat the Royal Engineers with one ball remaining of the 20 overs with James Hunt scoring 40 and Jon Sargent 46* who shared an opening stand of 110.
In the Semi-Final on Sunday 10th April, SCCC again scored over 200 with George Westlake ending on 75*, Jack Twigger racking up 52 and Tom Wright ending with 42.
In the final, SCCC overwhelmed a Spanish National team by 7 wickets with Lee Thomason and Pete McKay again in the runs with 58 and 40* respectively and Steve Howard taking 4 for 18.
The touring party returned safely to the UK with another trophy on 11th April, for the Awards Cabinet.
The Consultation Process
SCCC in conjunction with their Hockey Club colleagues have been seeking ideas on potential improvements to the facility from all of its members. Those that have responded to the Consultation Process have principally aimed their suggestions at an upgrade of both changing and shower facilities within the Pavilion.  These thoughts are now being collated and a plan of campaign for the future will be drawn up shortly by the General Purposes Committee (GPC) which operates on behalf of all members of the two Constituent Clubs and of our Associate Members (the Friends of Rectory Park [FORP]).  The GPC will publish its plans in due course and a programme of grant applications to potential Funders will be put into action, which will include the purchase of a new dedicated grounds equipment store. So, Watch this Space!
2016 Club Membership
The rules of the Birmingham and District Premier Cricket League together with those of the Warwickshire Cricket League require every player in either of the leagues to be a fully paid-up member of the Club which that player is representing.
In addition, since SCCC is a ClubMark club, we are also obliged to only select eligible players i.e. fully paid up during the current playing year. In addition to add to the problem with our selection process, SCCC also has a responsibility in respect of the Insurance cover that is provided for all players, whether junior or senior - the Club’s Insurance policy does not apply to any non-members who may wish to play for SCCC. Therefore, it is essential that, all players of whatever age are Club Members.
To ensure that every player is a legitimate Club member (each year), there is a requirement for everyone to complete a membership form as soon as possible (remember the SCCC membership year runs from 1st January each year to the 31st December). At the pre-season senior players’ meeting, everyone will be handed a membership form which SCCC requires be filled out and handed back to a Club Official (see list on page 4 of this Newsletter) together with the correct annual subscription or the information required for paying by staggered payments. Anyone not at the pre-season meeting will be handed a membership form by a Club Official before the season starts.
On and Off the Field Behaviour
New ECB guidelines have been issued about both the off and on-field behaviour of players. Because of a number of incidents involving both players from opposing teams and in some cases Umpires, the ECB has introduced a new Disciplinary Procedure which has been adopted by the Birmingham League.  This will ensure that any transgressions by players will be reported to the league when penalty points will be allocated to the offender and when that player or players reaches a specified level an automatic suspension will come into force.  In addition, the team Captain will also suffer the same suspension because of his or her inability to control the behaviour of his or her team members!
SCCC Club D6
The SCCC Club Day took place on 16th April 2016, starting at 9am – All existing players who have played for any of the senior teams in previous seasons or who may wish to play for any of the senior teams in 2016 were strongly urged to attend this Club Day which provides an opportunity for all players of whatever ability to get used to playing outdoors prior to the start of the competitive season, and to also stake a claim to one of the places in whichever SCCC team he or she may wish to play for.
Cricket Force Report
Cricket Force has become an annual event and so it was that on Saturday 3rd April some 25 people turned up to carry out a long list of essential tasks necessary to ensure that our Rectory Park facility is ready for the season. From varnishing benches, to painting the outside of the lower ground building and the score box, the tasks were many and varied. With Nat West’s support Cricket Force has become vital to hundreds of cricket clubs up and down the country and SCCC is no different. Further work was carried out on Sunday 4th April and the work carried over these two days when added to the huge amount of work carried out over the three previous Saturdays by a small group of volunteers, has meant that we are nearly ready for the season.
It was a great disappointment that so few current players turned up on any of the five days – our current playing members must take on board the fact that without the vast amount of pre-season work being carried out, there would be no cricket played (not just at SCCC but at all of the cricket clubs up and down the country)!
The Committee wants the success enjoyed by the first XI to continue on into the future and for this to happen, the amount of work required to raise the standard of ground facilities gets larger each year.
An Evening with two First Class Umpires
At the recent Umpires’ Evening which was really well attended by some fifty or more people, ICC Umpire Robin Bailey and ECB Umpire Nick Cook reminisced about their playing days and their subsequent move into umpiring with all that that task entails.
Amusing stories were interspersed with insights into the minds and actions of professional cricketers. Whilst all professional sportsmen have to be dedicated, professional cricketers are a breed apart. For them the work days are long and sometimes tedious as well as being tiring. To survive the rigours of the professional circuit, a player must either be totally dedicated or simply in love with the game. Robin and Nick obviously met both criteria – their love of the game shone through – and this was transmitted to the audience by their answers to every question thrown at them by the assembled gathering.
Paul Greetham was as usual, a more than adequate compere in the evening, leading the two Umpires through their careers and on to their ideas as to where cricket is going in the future, together with the latest ECB policy on player behavior both on and off the field.
It was a thoroughly enjoyable evening which acted as a great introduction to the forthcoming season.
Helmet Guidance
Please see the Home page of the SCCC website for the latest updated information on the wearing of helmets by cricketers.  If you are in any doubt about this information, please ask a member of the SCCC Committee for advice.  In essence, all seniors’ players (over 18yrs) are advised to wear helmets made to BS 7928:2013 when batting against all types of bowling, if keeping wicket when standing up to the stumps and if fielding in front of the bat within 8yds of middle stump.  Players under 18yrs of age must wear a suitable helmet (tested against the junior ball) at all times when batting or keeping wicket.
Obituary – Geoff Pritchett 1927 – 2016
Geoff Pritchett joined Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club in 1947 at the age of 20 as a wicket keeper batsman.  He played in the 2nd XI but by 1949 he was a first team regular appearing as an opening batsman but with George Swift retaining the wicket keeping job.  When Swift retired in 1954, Geoff became the first team keeper until Peter Lowe in 1958 who then took over the gloves. Geoff was able to concentrate on his batting, although he would keep wicket on any occasion when Peter Lowe wasn’t available.
Geoff scored 4 centuries for the 1st Team:
In 1953 - 106 at home against Leamington CC.
In 1954 - 113 not out away at Market Drayton CC.
In 1956 - 114 not out away at Ashfield CC.
In 1959 - 118 at home against Warwickshire Pilgrims CC.
After 1963 Geoff played for the 2nd team scoring two centuries: -
In 1977 - 102 not out at home against Pickwick Nomads CC.
In 1978 - 114 not out at home against Redditch CC.
In 1960 Geoff also scored 99 at Leicester Ivanhoe CC.
Geoff scored over 1,000 runs in two seasons: -
In 1953 he scored 1280 runs (914 on Saturdays & 366 on Sundays) and in 1957 he scored 1072 runs.
By 1970 Geoff had scored over 15,000 runs after which the annual figures were not recorded.  This figure does not include scores from the limited over cricket or Festival Shield, etc. Whilst keeping wicket for the 1st team, in 1954 Geoff held 14 catches and made 38 stumpings – a total of 52 victims. In 1955 he took 21 catches 21 and made 48 stumpings – a total of 69 victims and in 1956 he took 23 catches and made 44 stumpings – a total of 67 victims.
Geoff’s last season in the 1st XI was in 1962 when he scored 821 runs. This was his 15th season in the 1st XI. It is believed that Geoff retired as a player in 1978 during which season he scored 545 runs in the 2nd XI (his 17th season in the 2nd XI).
During his 31 seasons playing for SCCC Geoff was Captain of the 2nd XI for four seasons from 1969 to 1973 on both Saturdays and Sundays and he was Club Treasurer from 1966 until 1975 – a total of 10 Years.
In 1976 Geoff was elected a Life Member of SCCC and then was elected Club President in 1978 and he then finally retired in 1992 after 15 seasons as an Official of SCCC.
In a long and distinguished cricketing career, Geoff played a number of games for the Warwickshire County Cricket Club 2nd XI and was elected “Cricketer of the Year” in 1984 by the Midlands Club Cricket Conference (MCCC) – an award that was truly merited by this fantastic Sutton Coldfield CC Player and Official!
Geoff passed away recently in Norfolk, whence he had moved in the 1990s and when he handed over the SCCC Presidential reins to the late Peter Wells. SCCC offers a fond farewell to a great Clubman and extends its sympathy to Geoff’s family.
By Peter Bell
New Big Screen & New Equipment!
New equipment has been installed in the Cricket Pavilion and offer improvements to the facilities on offer to our members.  A new BIG SCREEN has been fixed to the wall of the Lounge area which will be used for showing Test Matches, County and ODI games, T20 games and for people interested in football, the Euros 2016. Come and see just how big the screen is!  Have a drink and relax in comfort watching England win (we hope!).
In addition, a new glass washer has been installed behind the bar together with two new chillers, there is a new freezer and new dishwasher in the kitchen and an enlarged laminate dance area has also been installed. (No studded boots allowed)
The Rectory Park facility has been in need of a revamp recently and the new additions to the infrastructure are part of the ongoing upgrade to the pavilion.
Friday Night Delight!
The first Friday evening junior practice of the season was well attended despite there being a bitingly cold wind blowing across the ground and the fact that the junior football season hadn’t yet come to an end which always draws some youngsters away from the beautiful game.  More than sixty juniors turned up on the 29th April which was a fantastic turnout.
By the second Friday (6th May) though the weather was superb and the result was a record attendance for any Friday evening at Rectory Park.
More than 130 youngsters attended what was a huge get together – this huge number of youngsters together with their parents and the people attending a junior football game behind the cricket ground caused a nightmare scenario on the car park.
Despite the hassle experienced by many parents when trying to find a parking space and then having to wait for a while when queuing to get a drink, everyone seemed to have a great time.  Our young cricketers of whatever age and ability all seemed to thoroughly enjoy their evening, and their parents enjoyed the chat and social gathering outside the pavilion. Altogether it was a brilliant evening – let’s hope for more excellent weather.
The Sutton Coldfield C.C. Story to The Royals Newsletter
After 36 additions of the Sutton Coldfield C.C. Story, which Phil Mist has produced since Issue No.1 first appeared on the 1st September 2012, Phil has decided to call it a day and has retired his pen after his last publication of May 2016.  It does help historians in having a regular supply of information to record for posterity, articles about the Club that you believe future generations will find to be of interest.  Thanks to Phil we have accurate information and data covering the last four years.  As one door closes another one opens, with Roy Hopwood having decided that he will produce a new editorial “The Royals Newsletter” and the first addition appeared on 1st July 2016.
New Wooden Five-bar Gate
The car park and the surrounding area has been the subject of apprehension for quite a while, with the problem of the container adding to these concerns.  It was decided that there was an urgent need to dispose of the container and tidy up the car park and surrounding area.  The idea of some form of a gate to provide access to our cricket ground for emergency vehicles and the Air Ambulance was muted some months ago and during conversations in the Pavilion a parent of one of our members kindly agreed to sponsor not only this much needed gate, but for all posts and fittings.  A magnanimous gesture of help and support.  Over the period of about two-week members from our Under15 and Under 19 teams, together with the Friends of Rectory Park carried out the work of installing the gate and fencing, a generous donation from another member.  The materials were purchased from the firm of Jewson, the builder’s merchant, utilising funds from a recently awarded ECB grant.
Ten Not Out Spectacular
On the 6th July 1916, a hundred years ago the battle of the Somme was fought in France with horrendous losses of British and Commonwealth Service Men, in which Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club lost 40 men out of 50 who enlisted into the Great War.  On 3rd July 2016 students from Little Sutton Primary School and Birmingham Metropolitan College, Sutton Campus in collaboration with Birmingham Hippodrome Creative put on ‘A Cricket Match Like No Other’.  Club and Team Members, family, friends and special guests were treated to a wonderful performance that switched from the playing field to the drama and horror of the battlefield.
On a rather warm but overcast afternoon students of Birmingham Metropolitan College, Sutton Campus and Little Sutton Primary School had been rehearsing for a very special performance. Members of the Birmingham Hippodrome were also there overseeing the event, in particular Creative Services Manager Liz Leck.
Inside the pavilion Stephanie, the Curator of the Royal Warwickshire Regimental Museum, had set up a display of memorabilia documenting the role of the regiment in First World War together with ‘hands-on’ items, such as replica uniforms, weapons and trench art.
In the kitchen were Karen, Bharat and their children who were our tea rota volunteers for the day.  Credit goes to them for providing a wonderful spread for both teams, making tea and coffee for people and for making sure that the kitchen was left clean and tidy.
Behind the bar John and his two-bar staff were already serving members, friends and their families.  There was another fine spread of food and cakes for all our guests in the function room, the cost of which had been donated by Waitrose  Club Open Days.  Here Warrant Officer (WOI) Mark Dufty, a 25-year veteran, medical engineer based at the Whittingham Barracks a cricket enthusiast, who found the event moving.
Outside family, friends, guests and members were gathering to watch the start of the cricket match – Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club XI v SCCC 2004 ‘Legends’ Championship winning team. The 1st X1 were first into bat and had the pleasure of being played on to the field by a solo violinist.  Whilst everyone was watching the game volunteers were bringing out a special treat – cakes, made by Julie Haines, in the style of the pre-1914 period.  Watching people’s faces light up with delight and then appreciation was, a testament to Julie’s baking skills.  She had also provided replicas of an early 20th century J Lyons & Co Ltd menu, which many people found interesting reading – especially the prices!
The skies cleared slightly for a while and the cricket pitch and spectators were bathed in brilliant sunshine.  By now the pavilion, outside seating area and surrounding fields were packed with people anxiously anticipating the long-awaited special event that was to be performed during the tea break.
The match was in full swing and, quite suddenly, there were a few gasps from people close to pavilion.  The cake had arrived, and what a cake! Made by the firm Cake Moda it was a true work of art replicating the image on Birmingham Hippodrome’s leaflet.  All eyes were upon it as the caterers carefully carried it into the pavilion and to a suitable area for photographing and then slicing.  The pavilion soon became full of people coming to admire, appreciate and take photos of this fantastic work of culinary art.
The first innings of the match had now ended and the teams were about to retire for their tea break, but first, the cutting of the cake, and the inevitable photo-shoot. This was performed by Peter Bell, Club Historian/Archivist, who was instrumental in initiating contact with the Birmingham Hippodrome.  He has dedicated much time and effort into researching the backgrounds of those gallant members of our Club who answered the call to arms in August 1914.  He, together with Liz Leck, Zara Harris, our Chairman, Colin Greetham and, without a doubt, many, many others are to be congratulated for organizing the spectacular event that was about to unfold.
After the cake-cutting, and allowing time for people to take photographs, The Chairman entered the field accompanied by students from Little Sutton Primary School. He explained
why and how this event was taking place and thanked everyone who had helped to bring this event together. Sutton schoolchildren, together with Daz from Bridge Arts and Music, then took center stage and sang a beautiful rendition, entitled ’50 Good Men’, that they had written.  The audience fell into an appreciative hush and I don’t think there was a dry eye to be seen during this moving performance.  What we do know is that this song will be professionally recorded and the school will sing it at their Armistice Day commemoration this year and in future years.
After a standing ovation in appreciation of the children’s singing the students of Birmingham Metropolitan College began their performance.  The narrator commenced his dialogue and the scene initially was a typical game of cricket; cricket bats and balls and fielders running to catch or stop a ball. Then, quite dramatically, a cricket ball was exchanged for a hand grenade.  The scene changed as the performer looked in confusion at this weapon of war whilst others performed their interpretation of the outbreak of hostilities and the first day of the Battle of the Somme.  The dancing and acting were superb as we were taken, in dance and narration, to that dreadful day and its inevitable culmination.
All around the audience was still and silent, save for gasps and comments of appreciation as we were taken through the sights and sounds of battle. The violinist then played ‘The Last Post’ as the Club’s flag was lowered in respect.  Before anyone realised, the performance ended and it took the audience a moment or two to return to the present before they erupted into loud applause and shouts of “Well Done”, “Splendid’ and “What a brilliant show”. The Chairman gave an emotional speech thanking all the participants, sentiments that were felt by everyone who had watched this truly superb performance.
Back inside the pavilion the crew of Big Centre TV were interviewing Peter Bell about the Club’s history and the fifty members who served in the military during the First World War.
Here’s what Peter had to say about his interview:
“I explained that I had become the Club’s historian and archivist, and had read in the minute book of 1914 - 1919 that 50 of our members had gone off on to the Great War and only 10 returned. Looking through the cricket score books of 1913 and 1919 checking names that were written in pencil and had faded plus the books had become very musty over the 100 years that had elapsed since they were filled in by a young school boy of 14, the late Norman Sharp who scored for the 1st XI Team.
Added to the problem was that no cricket was played in 1914 after the 8th August and the score books were never collected in from the teams’ captains. I had looked at every war memorial in Sutton to see what names appeared on them and were they recorded as having played cricket for Sutton. I only found 5 names and have written up obituaries on them, namely Percy Grove, Arthur Lilly, Septimus Parson, Horace Shenton and Edward Bagley Marwick. According to the Club’s minute book, ‘For many years an impressive Roll of Honour of those who served and gave their lives adorned the Pavilion, but unfortunately was destroyed by hooligans. No record of the names was ever recorded in writing’”.
A lot of interest had been shown in the Royal Warwickshire Regiment’s display and Stephanie explained to interested parties what they represented and the history of the Regiment.
A great deal of interest was also shown in the Club photographs, in particular that of the 1913 1st XI team and the Clubhouse when it was used for wounded soldiers to recuperate after being wounded. Others were eagerly queuing for a slice of the cake, and, judging by the appreciative smiles and comments, it was as good and delicious as it looked.
Throughout the remainder of the afternoon the game of cricket continued, people were enjoying both the match and the overall atmosphere.  There was no doubt in anyone’s mind that this day had been ‘A Cricket Match Like No Other’.
Tamworth’s Fame was Sutton’s Gain
The Government’s plan of l845 for the construction and extension of the railways posed a threat to the amenities of Sutton Park that had been enjoyed by many people, notably the Reverend Bedford and Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club. In 1847 Reverend Bedford decided to lay out new grounds in Rectory Glebes, now known as Rectory Park, as a new home for the Club.  Peter Bell, Club Historian & Archivist article for the Royals Newsletter No.1.
Sir Robert Peel of Tamworth’s fame was Prime Minister again after the 1841 general election.  This was his second government and ruled for 5 years, in which he introducing a number of major legislations, including Income Tax Act of 1842 and the Railway Regulation Act of 1844.  Birmingham in 1838 had been connected to London having been connected the previous year to both Manchester and Liverpool.  The rapid development of the railways was of some concern to all the residents in Sutton Coldfield who relaxed and enjoyed the tranquility of Sutton Park, as many had moved out of Birmingham in the early 19th century because, like most towns, Birmingham was considered dirty and unsanitary.
The Government’s plan of l845 was for the construction and extension of the railways, a position was therefore created whereby certain amenities in Sutton Park were threatened.  Among those enjoying the ambiance that Sutton Park offered was a small group that had played cricket in the park on the Meadow Platt for a number of years.  This resulted in the Rev Bedford, who, had been appointed Rector of the parish of Sutton Goldfield, laid out a cricket ground in the Rectory Glebes, now known as Rectory Park.  In 1847 the cricket club moved to their new ground under the shadow of the Rector's dwelling.  The parsonage was the home of the Bedford family for very many years and it was the hub around which all the early activities of the newly created Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club revolved. The Rector did a great deal to add beauty to Rectory Park.  He planted trees including a fine avenue of elms through which a distant view of the church could be seen from the Rectory windows. Sadly, only a couple of these fine trees still exist.  There still exists an Oak Tree of some 400 years old, hollow inside but still surviving and still a favorite hiding place for children.
Rectory Road, as we know it today, was merely a country lane running from Boot Hill to the church in almost a straight line, and it was the custom of the rector to walk to the church through the park, emerging by what the present generation known as "Sidney's Cottage" (the cottage on the north side of the pathway entrance to the park) now with a name plate stating “Rectory Cottage”.
Bedford Road, named after the family, was not constructed until about 1930, and in the early days was known as "Blackberry Lane" or "Bricky Lane" for it was only a track leading to the brick works and a few farms. "School Farm" frequently used to house members of visiting cricket teams, and the horse was the only means of transport other than by foot, and a game of cricket often depended upon the overnight hospitality of the home team. The "Tin Hut" which stood at the rear of the Boot Inn was erected for this purpose. But it has long since gone although some of the foundations are still there, if you are walking in the woods.
The rector also created a second cricket pitch for the “A” side on the other side of a two-foot-deep ditch that separated the two grounds.  This ditch was a hazard that many unsuspecting opposition fielders chasseing a ball heading for the boundary would find himself in water, if there have been rain a few days earlier.   At the end of the ditch was a small pond that flooded in winter, which has been dormant since the ditch was filled in.  Although the pond has reappeared in the last few years, probably due to the considerable number of changes in the park’s amenities that have occurred in recent years.
Sutton Coldfield station was finally constructed in 1862, and the Walsall to Water Orton line opened on the 1st July 1879 with stations at Pens Lane, Sutton Coldfield, Sutton Park, Streetly and Aldridge.  So, the Rector got it right.
200 NOT OUT
An aggregate of 200 Years of continual association with the Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club, known affectionately as the Golden Oldies. John was a quick bowler in the 1970’s-80’s who joined us from Liverpool.  Peter umpired for the 1st team game 1966 to 1998. Neil Houghton joined the Club in 1955 and was a quality batsman, an equally good off spin bowler and a very successful captained. While James, an Old Reptonian who had a distinguished school batting record, before joining the Club from school in 1971.
Free Foresters 160th Anniversary Match
On 20th July 1856 the Rev William Bedford founded the Free Foresters Cricket Club in the grounds of his rectory in Sutton Coldfield. The team is so named because ’Foresters refers to the Forests of Arden in Warwickshire and Needwood in Staffordshire, and ‘Free’ because members would be free to play against the club without permission, rather than being ‘tied’ to Free Foresters alone – hence their motto being ‘United though Untied’.
It was a somewhat overcast Sunday on 11th September 2016 when the Free Foresters returned to their inaugural ground to compete in a 160th anniversary match against a team of Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club (SCCC) players selected by match manager Andy Biddle.
Although still slightly overcast the weather remained good, and there was a good, friendly atmosphere created by plentiful support. SCCC went into bat first and, after a quality game of cricket, they won the match with a very notable century, scored by Lee Thomason. This being the main highlight of the match.
President’s Day 25th June 2016
The President's Day has had a poor track record with the weather, however this year the weather improved allowing an uninterrupted days cricket against top of the table Smethwick and we held out for a creditable draw.  Behind the scenes there was a great deal of hard work undertaken to enable everyone to have an enjoyable and convivial day.  Be it catering, the running of the bar, the preparation of the ground, window cleaning, collection of glasses and litter, cleanliness of the clubhouse and other tasks, many people are to be thanked for making the day a success.
A good crowd turned out and it was good to see our old player and Warwickshire’s leg spinner, Warwick Tidy who came up from Plymouth for his first visit for some years and was thrilled to see so many familiar faces that he played alongside in the 1970’s.  Also visiting us was Steve Grigg who now lives in Australia.
MCCC Presidents Day
Ken Lancaster, President of the Midland Club Cricket Conference chose Sunday 24th July to mark his Presidency with invites to 163 guests for the Sutton Coldfield C. C’s Warwickshire Sunday League’s game against Water Orton CC.  It was a warm and dry day with some 100-guest attended to watch the cricket and have afternoon tea.  It was good to meet up with many old friends from other cricket clubs and chat about old times in the past that we all enjoyed.  Sutton got the better of Water Orton who batted first in the 40 over a side game scoring 198 for 8 wickets.  Sutton in their reply scored 201 for 7 wickets with 4 balls to spare, a close encounter and a fitting result for a most enjoyable afternoons cricket.

The Chairman’s end of Season Report
I cannot report in such a celebratory manner as in the past three seasons, which seems unfamiliar and difficult to come to terms with. However, there is much to learn from our experience and we must respond positively in order to re‐establish the standards to which we have become accustomed.
It is clear that communications between management, players at all levels and coaches have to improve and if this happens, will go a long way towards improving matters Also, returning to strategies that proved successful when practicing and training. No one could have anticipated the number of injuries to squad members and this put great strain on availability and other selection issues.
La Manga was a new venture and the Club came away after winning the competition with plenty of good practice in pleasant surroundings. The optimism generated did not, unfortunately, continue for the rest of the season and soon dissipated as results began to go against us. All has not been ‘Doom and Gloom’ by any means, positive results being
Found elsewhere. The Junior Section again thrived with additional members making Friday and Monday nights very busy with record BBQ and bar returns.
The U19 and U12 sides both reached the playoffs of their respective competitions and a record number of Juniors represented the club in the various age groups. Junior players represented the County and District squads -­‐ all returning with creditable performances.
The Annual Dinner was particularly successful, as was Presidents Day, the Old Boys game and our involvement with ‘Ten Not Out’. We have hosted touring sides from Scotland and India, and have been the venue for school’s festivals whilst working hard to improve relations with members of the community.
The Free Foresters’ game was very successful -­‐ good weather, a good atmosphere created by plentiful support and quality cricket. The match was won by our Club side -­‐ a notable century scored by Lee Thomason being the highlight.
All that we do takes input, commitment and hard work from a large number of people.  The work that goes on ‘behind the scenes’ is considerable. My thanks and appreciation go to all those players, parents, committee members, sponsors, coaches and managers who have helped carry the Club on during this season. Teas are a particularly arduous duty and many volunteers filled the rota -­‐ our grateful thanks!
There is a great deal of hard work to be done to get the Club back on track. One season where we are not so successful is not catastrophic, but it may serve to remind us all that we cannot take things for granted and let complacency creep in. If we intend to play the game at the highest level then we take the lessons learned from 2016, pick ourselves up again and work to achieve the success that we have previously enjoyed.

Athletics within the Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club.
A lighthearted look at Athletics in the past by Peter Bell, Historian & Archivist.
Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club has had a tradition of athleticism within its membership and as early as Whit Monday in 1863 the Rector agreed with the Town Council to promote a sport event during a game of cricket in Rectory Park.   The event was advertised as “Club Feast” in Rectory Park, with prize money for the winner of the games, inter-mingled with “General Amusements - Country Dancing, Foot Ball, Cricket, Quoits and other Athletic Sports”.  Prize money was advertised as 2 shillings and 6 pence for the Hurdle Race winner for Men and also for the second Hurdle Race for Men, but excluding the winner of the first race.  There was also a 2 shilling and 6 pence prize for the winner of the Flat Race for Men.  Also in the programme were two Flat Races for Boys with a prize of 2 shillings and a similar prize for a Sack Race.  “Games to commence at three O’clock P.M. and All Disputes to be Referred to the Committee” There was no indication if ladies could take part in the sack race.  This event ran for a number of years but no records of winners were recorded and there was no accurate measured distance between the start and finish lines.
By 1871 there was a gravel track in Sutton Park alongside Coles Meadow, later to be known as Cristal Palace, close to where the Clifton Road Youth Centre and the ASTRO surface is now situated.  This was the first and only athletic sports event to take place amongst the Club’s members.  All the races, except the hurdle race were run on a gravel track, with the hurdles run on the grass.  It was recorded that there were some notable athletes amongst the members.  The Rev W.H. Colmore won the hundred yards and Godfrey Robinson won the 440 yards, referred to as the “quarter mile”, whilst the Rev W.C.R. Bedford finished second in both races but won the hurdles with Godfrey Robinson finishing in second place.
Two years later, representatives of these families all gained "Blues".  W.C.R. Bedford and T.M. Colmore ran the hundred for Cambridge and Oxford, respectively, whilst G.M. Robinson rowed in the Cambridge boat.  John Ellison for many years the "Starter" at the Bishop Vesey's school sports was also a participant in the events at Coles Meadow.
We also had an outstanding athletic in Eric Bertram Crockford known as Tom who played cricket for Sutton Coldfield CC and Warwickshire CCC and won a gold medal in the 1920 Olympic games in Antwerp playing hockey for Great Britain and Ireland, plus he represented England on 17 occasions.  His indoor activates including billiards where he was an exceptional good player holding a number of records for the highest break.
In the summer of 1936, the Olympic Games were held in Berlin and we can boast of success by another Club member, namely, Arthur Godfrey Kilner Brown who lived at 146 Rectory Road and won a gold medal in the 4 x 400-meter team relay and a silver medal in the 400-meter individual race.  Godfrey’s sister Audrey also won a silver medal at the 1936 Olympic Games, known as the “Hitler Games” and it is worthy of noting that Godfrey’s brother Ralph, who also played cricket for the Club, later to be knighted as Sir R.K. Brown won the 1934 AAA 440 yards hurdles title and finished third in the Empire Games later that season.
Today’s cricketers are extremely athletic, as no ball is safe until it hits the boundary line because fielders will dive or slide tackle the ball to prevent it crossing the line.  Players of the past never or rarely got grass stains on their flannels because they had to be dry-cleaned, whereas cricket whites of today can be washed and back in use within hours.
Chairman Reports
The Outgoing Chairman, Colin Greetham of Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club Final Report to the Annual General Meeting of Monday 7th November 2016
----------------------------------------------------------------------------
By recent standards this past season has been very disappointing.  To arrive at this conclusion is relatively easy, by analysing the stats and finding ourselves playing in Division Two in 2017 emphasizes the point.  The First Eleven were not good enough!  But before we come to believe that, consider circumstances prevailing...........team squad members were the same as in 2015, but no one who has been a long-standing member here can remember the number of serious injuries to players as in 2016. This had a knock-on effect not only to the First Eleven but also to the other sides.
Other factors affected team performance.......loss of form......poor attendance at practice.......inconsistent availability.......unfortunate personal circumstances.
Undoubtedly the team members failed to gel as in past.  The breakdown of effective communication between teams and Management, which undoubtedly affected team moral and ethos.
Trying to analyse the situation in great depth is a waste of energy which can be put to more effective use, but the mistakes must be recognized and acted upon if we are to pick up again. We have talented players, good coaches and strategies which have brought success.....why try to fix what isn't broken?
The Club should play at the highest level possible, providing coaches to develop players at every level and bring youngsters through to enable them to challenge for first team cricket. BDPCL is due for reorganisation in the very near future...to play in that League must be a main objective. Surviving at that level depends on the Junior section developing our youngsters and providing talented cricketers to supply a feeder stream into the senior sides. Also, the senior players must be mentally prepared and talented enough to compete at that level.
I am pleased to say that the Club is generally in good shape.....we have very good facilities due to be enhanced by the addition of a new ground store (the removal of the containers will tidy up the surrounds).  A sound bank balance, group of enthusiastic sponsors, committee structure which is effective (given all posts are filled), good social and changing facilities, training facilities both winter and summer and a development plan which gives good direction. The junior section has increased numbers attending Friday training, supplying the Club with a steady flow of youngsters who are eager to learn to play and funds which enhance the bank balance. Sides are regularly playing in the Warwickshire Leagues often challenging, as this year in the playoffs for County titles.
To move the Club forward, our communication at all levels must improve.  That is why it is pleasing to see a new committee position which will bring aspects of communication closer together and hopefully be more effective.  It is challenging but not beyond our means to organise communication systems which embrace all levels of membership and regularly update on important issues. Players, Coaches, Committee Members and others, must maintain an open dialogue so that important issues can be fully discussed.
In order to continue the process of developing the Club, providing a sporting and social facility to be proud of and encouraging the community to recognize and become part of what we do, personnel are always required to help manage the organisation.  Fresh, young, enthusiastic people with new ideas are needed to keep the Club moving forward.  This is no more necessary than at the present time when playing the game, although most important....is only part of what is required to keep a Club such as ours functioning.  I ask you all to consider giving some of your valuable time to help in this way.
There number of volunteers who give valuable service to the Club in many areas, such as; preparing teas; Friday. BBQ and admin; helping on the ground; coaching and managing Junior sides. All these folk work very hard and our sincere thanks go to them. There is always room for others to come on board and lend a hand.
Since becoming a member over 25 years ago, I have always held a position of some responsibility.  I wish to remain a member of the Club and help wherever possible but without the responsibilities of being a committee member.  It is time for me to hand the baton over, so with effect from the AGM on 7th November 2016, I have resigned as Chairman of Sutton Coldfield Cricket. I have done my best to move the Club forward.  By and large we have been very successful over the past five years. I am confident this can and will continue.
I wish the Club all the very best for the future and express sincere thanks to those who have worked closely with me and supported the developments which have brought the Club to its present position. There is plenty more to come and look forward to seeing the Club becoming an even greater force in local cricket.
Best wishes,       Colin Greetham

Chairman’s Address to the Members at the AGM of 7th November 2016
I am extremely proud to have been elected as Chairman of Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club and hope to carry on the excellent work of Colin Greetham.  I would personally like to thank Colin for all of his hard work and excellent leadership over the past 5 years as Chairman.  I am fortunate to be supported by an excellent and experienced committee who are all committed to continuing the development of Sutton Coldfield as a place where all with a love of cricket are welcome and to providing a high-quality venue to both play and watch the game.
Over the coming weeks I am meeting with all my colleagues to agree our priorities for moving the club forward.  Even though it is the closed season, much work needs to be done to ensure the ground is prepared for next season, captains are appointed, winter practice arrangements are finalised and so forth.
In particular Roy Hopwood our Communications Manager and I will be sitting down to review how we communicate effectively to all our players and supporters. We are keen that communications are a two-way process, and would be pleased to hear from any of you who have ideas on how we are operating as a club.  As a committee we are here to represent you, our members.  I hope over the coming months to meet as many of you as possible to hear what you have to say about the way the club should be run.
We are keen to involve more people in the running of the club.  We have several committee roles that remain unfilled but I am also keen to hear from anyone who is happy to be involved on an ad-hoc/more informal basis.  If you are prepared to help in any capacity, in the first instance, please contact me via the club email address
Kind regards,        Steve Russell
Four Plus Nine = Thirteen
Continuing the Ten Not Out theme a small working party of Liz Leck, (Hippodrome Theatre) Jo Brookes, (Researcher) Roy & Doreen Hopwood, Colin Greetham, James Ross, Dave Phillips (BVGS) & Peter Bell got together with the aid of a grant to search military records, Censes documents, the historical Sutton Coldfield News and the Club’s historical documents of the 1914 to 1919 period.  We appear to have found 40 that joined the services during the 1914 - 1918 conflict and a total of 13 paid with their lives.  Obituaries of the nine new identities have been written up and are shown below the list of all the 39 we have identified as having served in the forces during the Great War.
The Warwickshire Sunday League
Formed in 1984 to provide a competitive cricket for Warwickshire based clubs interested in playing league cricket on Sundays.  However, over the recent seasons the enthusiasm for Sunday cricket has deteriorated, mainly due to the B&DPL’s fixtures requiring clubs with excessively long travel distances to and from their league away ground when meeting for away games is in some cases around 9 AM with arrival back some 12 hours later.
At the WSL AGM of 24th October 2016, it was proposed and accepted that the Executive would approach the Arden Sunday League to enquire on the possibility of the members in the WSL could join the ASL.  The Management of the ASL stipulated that each club would have to apply.  At the convened EGM the WSL clubs voted to apply to join the ASL, whereupon the WSL ceased as a league for Sunday Cricket for Warwickshire Clubs.

Annual Dinner - Another Success!
The Club's Annual Dinner on Friday 10th February 2017 was a great success.  Similarly to recent dinners it was well supported and the staff at Moor Hall Golf Club provided excellent food and service as usual. Compered by our President, James Ross, the evening started with Jez Jones and Ann Jones from Bridge Arts & Music performing '50 Good Men', one of the songs from the brilliant Ten Not Out event at the Club in the summer.
Following dinner, the £200 'stand-up-bingo' prize was won by great friend of the Club, Reg Brassington.
Guest speaker Chris Grant then spoke about some of the financial challenges facing county cricket. For the past 6 years Chris has been the Chairman of Derbyshire County Cricket Club and will shortly leave the post to seek election to the to the ECB board. Chris gave a fascinating picture of the county scene and our thanks go to him and former Derbyshire wicketkeeper Tom Poynton for coming along.
Chris and Tom had earlier presented prizes to Rob & Julie Haines (Club Persons of the year), Ben Dunnington (Carol Hansford Trophy), Rahim Khalil (Terry Matthews Award).
The specially commissioned “Ten Not Out” trophy was presented to John Robinson on behalf of Peter Bell who was unfortunately absent as his wife Sue was receiving treatment for cancer at the QE Hospital. project created mutually beneficial links between all parties involved.

Roger then auctioned items for the Club - a golf day at Moor Hall, a signed Warwickshire shirt and a 4-litre bottle of scotch whiskey signed by England Rugby Union international James Haskell.
"Ten Not Out" by James Ross
"In 2014 Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club senior and Life Member, Peter Bell thought it an appropriate time to investigate just how many of the club's members had fought in The First World War and how many had lost their lives.  There had previously been a memorial in the pavilion which had been vandalised approximately eighty years ago and no records now currently existed.
Present on the day were many spectators, Mark Duffy Warrant Officer of the Royal Warwickshire Regiment and artefacts from their museum.  The Last Post was played complete with the lowering of the club flag which created a memorable and atmospheric afternoon.On-going was the research of those members who had both served and died.  This was undertaken with the assistance of historians Jo Brookes and Ariadne Plant, Bishop Vesey's Grammar School archivist David Phillips, genealogists Roy and Doreen Hopwood and club members, Peter Bell, Colin Greetham and James Ross.  The eventual outcome was that thirty-nine members went to war and fourteen did not return.
The project had many benefits.  The realisation that in the history of Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club since 1847 there can have been no historical event that had so much emotional impact on its community.  It created much awareness amongst its current membership as these losses meant that the club nearly didn't have the manpower to reconvene in 1919.  The Royal Warwickshire Regiment were able to add further information to their archives.  Little Sutton Primary School children had an invaluable history lesson, enhanced knowledge of life for their relatives a century ago and the creation of a song.  "Fifty Good Men" is to be their school song at every Remembrance Day henceforth.  The project created mutually beneficial links between all parties involved.
Birmingham Hippodrome in the guise of Hippodrome Creative under the direction of Liz Leck contacted Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club with a view to expanding the project within the local community.  Little Sutton Primary School years five and six were brought into the project to enhance their appreciation of history a century ago, the futility of war, and the impact on their families only a few generations ago.
Initially it was thought that fifty members of Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club went to war and only ten men returned.  Hence the naming of the project, "Ten Not Out!"  The first part of the project was for Little Sutton Primary School to create a song that would be a lasting memorial.  Daz and Ann of Bridge Music and Arts teamed up with the school and the song they composed was "Fifty Good Men."  This was then sung at an outdoor dramatization during the interval at a match at Rectory Park home of Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club.
Peter cuts the cake
To mark the occasion, Liz Leck had organised a large cake, showing a cricket bat and ball with a hat on top and inscription of Ten Not Out.
A sculpture was commissioned with Lottery funding which is a unique piece of modern art commemorating the bravery of those Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club members who lost their lives in World War One.  It has been presented and will be awarded in future to a member for outstanding achievement. It sits proudly as a lasting and touching memorial at our home in Rectory Park.  “Appropriately the first recipient was Peter Bell."
Salvete & Valete
The Club are particularly pleased to welcome back Matt Parker who as well as adding to our 1st XI squad this season, brings his skills as a coach and in strength and conditioning available to parents as well as juniors in that particular aspect.  Welcome also to our new overseas player Dinushka Nawarathnage, a 23-year-old all-rounder from Sri Lanka, (subject to Home Office approval).  Dinushka is currently playing first class cricket for the Royal Naval Club in his home country and is expected to arrive in Sutton around mid-April and is expected to play throughout the summer and will also be involved in coaching on Tuesdays and Fridays and will also be available for 1-2-1 sessions with juniors.
Dinushka arrival is a replacement for Dan Childs, who has an opportunity of a teaching post in his home country of South Africa.  Something that was too good to pass over and will also allow him to concentrate of finishing his teaching diploma, which was one of his goals and he is also getting married to Aletta in October and we wish him well.  For those of you interested, Dan has also fully recovered from his back problem and recently took a 6-wicket haul to help his side to go top of the league with two games to go.  Dan contributed significantly to the club’s success over his 5-year spell here and I hope it's not the last time we see him at Rectory Park.
Over the winter month a number of players have depart for pastures new for a variety of reasons.  One of the challenges we face as all club do is that our talented players are often targets for other clubs willing to pay reasonable sums of money to entice players away. This unfortunately has been the case with a couple of our leavers.  On the plus side players leaving create opportunities for others to join the Sutton Coldfield Club and a number of players have moved to the club from Aston Unity and will undoubtedly strengthen our teams particularly at 3rd team level where our lack of experience has seen us struggle over the last 2 seasons.  It will also allow our younger players to take the chances they are given. This will however also mean that our more experienced players have a key role in supporting and mentoring those less experienced at senior level.
One notable departure was Steve Perryman who had a distinguished playing and coaching career with both Warwickshire and Worcestershire County Cricket Clubs has decided not to continue as Head Coach.  Steve has played an important part in the development of our club over the last few years and we thank him for his efforts and wish both he and Carol well for the future.
The Groundsman’s Storage replacing the Groundsman’s Shed.
It is about 10 years since the wooden groundsman’s shed collapsed and the ugly tin cans arrives as a short-term measure and have appeared to be permanent on both the ground and car park, so by the next newsletter that will have gone as the new building is in the final stage of completion.  Nobody will mourn the departure of the containers as they have been an eye sore and subject to many unkind comments from all who visit Rectory Park.  So, we are deeply indebted to the Municipal Charities for providing much of the funding and to our main sponsors by the Lane Group who have project managed the build.  The Construction will see an end to the unsightly containers in the bottom corner of the ground and in the car park whilst providing better storage for our equipment.  The building has a concrete base and is in place between the pavilion and the score box and the store itself has been constructed of steel and is of a dark green colour.  The Club has worked hard over recent years in finding the funding for the storage of our expensive ground machinery and the new building will enable us to store and maintain our equipment in a more suitable place.
A View from the Past for the Newsletter
Many of the older members will remember the 'clip clop' sound of the daily deliveries of goods being delivered to their homes, such as bread, milk, coal and even a Davenports Beer at Home service.  All the carts were pulled by either a Suffolk Punch or a Clydesdale, who were horses with big feet and powerful shoulders. The next time you are likely to see a Clydesdale is when you watch the State Visit of the United States of America's President, Donald Trump, in which the Household Cavalry drum horse will be a Clydesdale.
The Cricket Club had horses up until around the start of the Second World War during which very little cricket was played and after the war the Club secured a small Ferguson tractor.
Looking back at the minutes of the first half of the Twentieth Century, the Club was never very kind to the horse with quotations that “the horse will last another season” and “it is lame but it should last the season”. The horse was, however, an integral part of the groundsman's equipment. Every morning the horse would have to be dressed, not just with his or her harness, but two pairs of steel shoes with leather straps were put on to prevent the horse damaging the playing surface. It was quite a chore for the horse to be persuaded to lift each leg in turn to fit the shoes on correctly. The horse was then connected to the very heavy roller and for the next hour or so the horse and groundsman would traverse the next cricket strip to be played on, requiring a large turning circle at both ends so as not to damage the square. At the end of the cricket season the horse would be part of the ground rent agreement with Sutton Coldfield Hockey Club for rolling the pitches after hockey games. The shoes remained in the back of the old wood groundsman's shed until around 2000 when the shed finally collapsed and was replaced with the tin cans on both the ground and in the car park. At last, the tin cans will disappear when the new brick groundsman's store, currently under construction will accommodate the entire Club's ground equipment.
The documents always referred to the horse as “The Horse” it never had a name nor was it described as a mare or stallion, yet the horse was part of the Club's ground work for nearly one hundred years.  An incident in 1912 in the minute’s states that 'the horse being sacked for kicking the groundsman'. I suppose he may have had a stone in his shoe.
The horse lived outside in Rectory Park all the year, although there was a bit of a lean-to by the groundsman's shed. However, if there was any severe frost around during the winter months and it was obvious than no hockey was playable then the horse would be sent back to the local farmer to be under cover.  Today's children will go up to a horse and you can see them leaning on the railings being encouraged by parents to give the horse a 'tit bit' of some sort, like an apple or carrot, but from the 1900s to the 1940s the public kept away from the horse because of its unknown temperament.
Most of the younger members of today regard 'Horse Power' as something you consider when buying a car whilst looking under the bonnet, but, to the older members, the sound of the 'Clip Clop' of the awaited delivery of essential goods is remembered with nostalgia.
By Peter Bell, the Club Historian & Archivist who wrote the following verse for the local newspaper.  “Remembering the past, with hope for the future.”
I remember the Coal man, Milkman and Baker’s vanand counting the coal bags being left by a black man,but not in colour, it was only dustthat appeared on his face like a crust.
Our coal deliveries were hauled by a Clydesdaleand carried into your home by a man who was not frail.There was also Davenports “Beer at Home” was his shoutas he entered your road offering you ale or a bottle of stout.
Then there were the fireworks, day and by night,with the sky lit up by search lights,as bangers went off, giving you a fright,by German bombs producing an appalling sight
With our ration book to handOur grocer would demandcoupons for sugar, butter, bacon and eggs,plus, another for boiled sweets or even clothes pegs
The only orange I knew about was not round,but came from a bottle, under the counter found,for children in need of vitamin “C”that doctors had arranged to see.
CCTV Cameras
As part of the ongoing improvements to the clubhouse, a new CCTV system was installed last week giving greater coverage of the area including the new ground store, and also includes two new internal cameras. One camera is in the lounge, and the other one is in the dining room. These have been installed to support the club's application to change to a    Premises.
The club has registered the new system with the Data Protection Register, and it will only be used if an incident is reported and needs investigation.
Groundsman Course
During the month of February Mike Hepburn, Grounds Chairman, went on a Groundsman Course in Bristol and on his returned has indicated that maintenance will be carried out on the nets, and they will be treated and industrially cleaned.  Mike also sent an urgent appeal for volunteers to undertake a number of maintenance tasks as there is a great deal of essential work required on the ground before the start of the season.  Over the winter months a small grounds team have been busy removing leaves from the ground, and with the aid of a skip the stored clubhouse rubbish has been cleared out and a general tidying up achieved, so many thanks to all those volunteers.   The grounds team have also treated and spiked the squares and the outfield will shortly be slit, spiked and weed killer will be applied in time for the start of the new season.
Good Bye Unsightly Storage Containers
The storage containers on the ground, used to store the Club’s ground equipment have now at last gone with the new groundsman’s storage facilities in full use.  This will also release a changing room within the pavilion that has for necessity being holding expensive ground equipment for safe keeping for many years.  Also, the last container that was situated in the car park was removed, much to the pleasure of the FORP and visitors to the park.  The BCC also resurfaced the roadway into the car park and put down double yellow lines on it, which has made access for all vehicles easier and safer.
Fixture Cards
In the haste to get this season’s fixture cards into circulation they were not “Prof Read” and had to be reprinted as some 23 errors were identified including many of the sponsors’ advertisement regarding their company activities.
In Memory of the Battle of Passchendaele & Albert Edward Wakefield
Between July and November 2017, the 100th anniversary of the Third Battle of Ypres, which became known as the battle of Passendaele was fought out in 1917 during the First World War.  For the soldiers who fought at Passchendaele, it was known as “The Battle of Mud”.
Passchendaele as it was called in 1917, is a rural Belgian village in the Zonnebeke municipality of West Flanders.  During three and a half months of fighting which cost the lives of over half a million Allied and German service men were wounded or lost.  Around 90,000 British and Commonwealth soldiers were missing, of which 50,000 were buried without being identified and 42,000 bodies were never recovered from the Belgian fields of Flanders that turned into an ocean of mud.
Among those that lost their life and whose body was never recovered was a young man of 20 years of age, who lived in Mere Green Road with his parents, brothers and sisters and played for the Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club’s “A” team.  He was Albert Edward Wakefield, born in Boldmere, Sutton Coldfield on 27 October 1896 and was the son of William Alfred and Florence Wakefield who moved to Mere Green Road, Four Oaks in 1899.  He was educated at Bishop Vesey Grammar School 1908 to 1910, and on the 29 September 1910 transferred to the Town Boy’s School.
Albert enlisted in Sutton Coldfield sometime in 1914 with the Norfolk Regiment – Private 32133 and later he served with the Worcestershire Regiment 1/7th Battalion – Private 41382.  Why Albert was transferred to the Worcestershire Regiment is not known
On 16 August 1917 the 1st/5th Gloucester’s of the 48th Division (to which the 1st/7th Worcestershire’s belonged) struggled to capture the last house in the north of St. Julian, a village about 5 miles north east of Ypres.  Albert Wakefield was killed when a company of Worcester’s launched an unsuccessful attack on Maison du Hibou at 2.30 am on the 17th of August. The fighting had been fierce in an attempt to capture the village of St Julien just to the south of Langemarck and they were caught in German crossfire from Hillock Farm and Maison du Hibou.  Hillcok Farm eventually fell to the Bucks Regiment, 1/1st Battalion.  Everywhere there were bodies, English and German, in all attitudes and stages of decomposition.
Albert’s death was reported as killed in action on 17 August 1917 (France & Flanders) and he has no known grave and was awarded the Victory and British Medals.
Albert is commemorated on the Tyne Cot Memorial and on the Bishop Vesey Grammar School’s Roll of Honour.  He is also commemorated on the St James Church, Mere Green, Roll of Honour where he worshipped and a Memorial Service was held at St James Church in his memory.
No army service record can be found on-line of Albert’s career serving King and Country prior to the battle of Ypres.  However, he did have two brothers; Percy, who was 3 years older than Albert and in the 1911 census at the age of 16 he was employed by the LNWR (London & North Western Railway) as a porter.  Albert’s younger brother, Arthur Stanley Wakefield who in the 1939 census would be around 44 years of age and was living at Church View in Mere Green Road with his Mother Florence Wakefield who would be around 74 years of age when the 1939 census took place.
Albert had three younger sisters according to the 1911 census living in Church View, Mere Green Road; Hilda Victoria, Dorothy Rose and Irene Florence.  In the 1939 Census, Dorothy Rose Wakefield would be about 36 years of age and unmarried and was still living in Church View, Mere Green Road with her brother Arthur and her mother.  What became of the Wakefield family?  It would be interesting if anyone has any information on this family, in particular Arthur who died in battle 100 years ago on the 17th August 2017.
By Peter Bell
411 runs for 2 wickets
Looking back over the 170 years that the Sutton Coldfield Cricket Club have been playing cricket, Saturday the 8th July was the first occasion that any Sutton team has passed the 400-run mark in any sort of cricket; this was achieved away at Harborne’s Old Church Avenue ground.  A ground that has always been a little smaller than most ground, where traditionally big scores have been registered but none that I can recall of over 400 runs.  Not even when the Warwickshire County Cricket Club took a very strong side over to play Harborne in what was their centenary year in 1968 a game that I umpired.
However, Sutton’s game at Harborne was an outstanding achievement by the team in scoring 411 runs for the loss of two wickets in which the Club picked up 17 points and up into 3rd position in the league.  The game was a personal success for two young players; Captain & wicket keeper Lee Thomason (24 years of age) and Tom Eustace (22 years of age): who in an unbroken 3rd wicket stand of 290 runs in which Lee was not out with 202 runs to his name and Tom on 127 when the 55 over were completed by a very tied and exhausted Harborne team.
Lee only received 147 balls and scored 11 sixes and 18 fours, with Lee’s second 100 being scored off 32 balls.  Tom was no slow coach, joining Lee with the score at 121 when Mark Guest was out on 59, scoring his 127 runs off 84 balls with 5 sixes and 16 fours.  Lee and Tom between them caused Harborne to have to find 9 replacement balls.
For those interested in statistics, a total of 17 sixes = 102 runs and 47 fours = 188 runs totaling 390 runs scored in boundaries in the Sutton innings, which included Mark Guest 11 fours and 1 six in the 81 balls he received and Amir Shabir in an opening partnership of 26 with Mark scored 2 fours.
Harborne in their reply managed to score 219 runs for the loss on 8 wickets with our overseas player Dinushka Nawarathnage taking 4 wickets for 62 runs in 12 overs and James Lunn taking 3 for 25 runs in 13 overs.  Steven Howard chipped in with a wicket in his 12 overs for 62 runs, with Harborne well short of Sutton’s total by 192 runs.
An outstanding achievement for the Sutton 1st team and a copy of the score card will be recorded in the 170 years of the Club’s History.
Highlights of the Season
A season that promised much with the 1st team being in 4th position in the BC&DPL Division 2 for a number of weeks in the early part of the season but with players availability diminishing regularly and the with the highest number of abandoned games; 5 in total; in all BCHUA fixtures the team finished the season in 9th position, but were never in danger of being relegated.
In the Leagues statistics, only a few members were mentioned.  Lee Thomason finished in 9th position in Division 2 with a batting average of 75.6 and was runner up with a total of 878 runs for the season.  Lee also hit the highest individual in all B&DPL clubs with his 202 not out against Harborne.
In the bowling our overseas player from Sir Lanka, Dinushka Nawarathnage finished 8th in the Division 2 leading wicket taker with 31 wickets and an average of 25.8.  His best performance was against Fordhouses where he took 6 wickets for 25 runs.
Jack Smith got a mention for finishing 6th in the league with 13 catches.

Oswestry and Halesowen were promoted to Division 1 for next season, with Old Hill and Old Elizabethans being relegated into Division 3.  Incidentally Walmley CC were relegated from the 1st Division and will join the Sutton Coldfield Club in Division 2 next season.
The second Xl played in the B&DPCL 2nd Division South and finished the season in 9th position out of the 12 team with 198 points.  No players appeared in the leagues average in either, batting, bowling or fielding statistics.
The 3rd Xl playing in the 10th Division of the Warwickshire Cricket League finished the season in 5th position out of the 10 clubs that played in the Division 10 West with a creditable number of league points of 209. Rhyn Morgan finished the season in 13th position in the league’s averages with an aggregate of 305 runs and average of 33.7 and his highest score was 63.  Sam Bogle finished in 19th position and has a highest score of 106 and an aggregate of 238 runs at an average of 34.0.  In the bowling Ramanjit Singh finished in second position in the league with an average of 11.86 and a total of 28 wickets with a best performance against Shenely Field CC with 5 for 10 in 5 overs.
A Sutton Coldfield 4th team played in the WCL Division 12 finishing 6 out of 8 teams with Martin Sprawson finishing 4 in the division’s batting averages with 44.4 and a highest score of 79 and totaled 222 runs in the season.
Arden Sunday Cricket league
With the closure on the Warwickshire Sunday League the Sutton Coldfield 1st team joined the Arden Sunday Cricket league and were placed in the 3rd Division.  Incidentally there was no 1st Division but two Premier Leagues Blue and Yellow. The team finished the season as runners up to Kenilworth Wardens with 182 points, but no player appearing in either the batting or bowling top 10 averages.
